Yeah, I’m an alkie, he’d say (using the cutesy nickname so that the asker would understand that he was seasoned, like an old sage), but I’m a functional alkie, y’ see?

A disciplined alkie. > Howzatt?

The asker would usually respond blearily.

This person was generally drunk out of his or her guord; one generally had to be, to be ask a perfect stranger personal questions.

In his head, Bill secretly thought they were complete assholes for asking, but he would never say that to them out loud.

A customer was a customer, no matter how much of an asshole they might be, and if there was one thing that had kept The Fish Store open and busy for the past twenty-nine years straight, it was that particular philosophy.

In Bill’s humble opinion, there was nothing more important in the entire world than a repeat customer.

A bunch of the barflies had decided to get together and lay bets on the game, despite Bill’s efforts to quash the idea.

He’d run this bar since the day it opened in June of fifty-five, and he knew how these things went.

Viscera, being Viscera, climbed it for “communion.” He put the Beta 58 between his teeth and leaned into the crowd like a saint at a meat raffle.

The barricade groaned.

Jules pointed at him with all five fingers, the universal sign for GET OFF THE RAIL.

He smiled, licked the grill ball like a spiteful cat, and stepped back just in time for three surfers to come over like lumber.

The pyro cue was supposed to be a polite CO2 burp, but the timecode hiccuped—frame-rate mismatch on an imported session—and the tech got **both** CO2 and a surprise concussion blast.

The LD’s face reflected the fire like an ancient priest.

The crowd howled like a hunting party.

The city fine wrote itself in the air in invisible ink.

Two songs from the end of the main set, the cheap barricade finally surrendered.

The security captain blew his whistle.

House lights to half.

Jules cut throat with her hand: STOP.

The band crashed to a halt.

For three beats, silence like a held breath.

Then Viscera, wordlessly, jumped down with security, hauling the metal back like a farmer dragging fence through mud, yelling “Back!

The room, shamed, took a step.

In the gap, they reset stanchions with zip-ties and bad faith.

He scrambled back up, grabbed his mic, and said, “Nobody dies at my show.” Which the local news would later edit to “Nobody dies at my show—tonight,” because irony is a producer.

They finished the set with a tempo that sounded legally actionable.

Offstage, Viscera’s hands shook like Morse.

He blew them out, stared at his notebook like it had betrayed him, and laughed.

House wanted the encore done by 22:58.

Second encore song, bridge section.

Later he’d say it was a tone.

Right then it just looked like a man falling into a well.

He let the band ride the loop and walked to the mic.

He looked out over the pit, the balconies, the hung steel and sweating lights, and the black jaws of the loading dock.

He smiled the famous smile people would later argue was a flinch. “Wouldn’t this be a great night,” he said, casual as a bartender, “to end it all?” He meant: end the set.

End the ongoing joke where the world is a machine you crank until a melody falls out.

What 4,000 people heard was a promise.

A second of stunned laughter.

A second more of cheers shaped like knives.

Then the room’s breath changed.

The balcony chant began soft—“Don’t you dare”—and the pit answered with “Do it,” because mobs write their own harmony.

The band, professionals in a storm, kept the loop going like morticians that smile.

FOH brought the mains down a whisper.

Jules stared at him with a look that said You will not give him the quiet he wants.

He brought them back.

He realized precisely what he’d said, and the thought registered in his pupils like a cloud across the sun.

He tried to pull it back.

He always does. “Relax,” he grinned, too many teeth. “I mean the night.

I mean this *song* dies so you can go sin.” He held the note out like a bridge over a canyon.

The room didn’t take it.

On the rail, a kid burst into tears.

On the balcony, a dude climbed onto the concrete lip again, arms out, daring gravity to argue.

Security didn’t see him.

He pointed. “Don’t,” he said into the mic, and the word came out of the PA like a sermon.

They ended on a held chord and a blizzard of cheap confetti the promoter had insisted on buying because “we need pictures.” The confetti got sucked into the dimmer fans and burned.

It smelled like sugar death.

Backstage, because stories do not respect curfews, a stagehand found a phone abandoned behind a stack of subs with the camera still rolling.

An alt-weekly tweeted it with a caption about mental health.

A blog reframed it as a threat.

By sunrise, a pastor in Nebraska would preach about him by name.

By noon, the label would call the TM to ask if he needed “support” or a muzzle.

Settlement in the promoter’s office was fluorescent and cruel.

He stood in the doorway, vibrating, while the rep argued about overages on barricade repair and the noise fine and the extra security call.

The rep tried to claw back $12,400 from our backend.

Our TM slid the force majeure clause across the table and reminded him they’d oversold the room.

Everyone smiled like divorce.

Luke tried to sell Viscera on the idea of turning the quote into a shirt.

Viscera walked past him silent, through the bus, into the back lounge, shut the door, and wrote in his notebook until the paper ripped.

On the curb, Jules taped a new day sheet over the old: **Load-in: 09:00.** It flapped in the exhaust wash like a white flag.

Somewhere, already, a columnist typed that he was a coward for not doing it.

Somewhere else, a fan typed that he’d saved her because when he said *Don’t* she heard it in her own kitchen.

If you wanted to trace the twenty years that followed—the riots, the cancellations, the book that misquoted him, the security briefings that always opened with a mention of “ideation,” the way every cop who met him for the next decade looked into his eyes the way men look at open windows—if you wanted to mark where it all began, you could do worse than a concrete room in Everett on a night when the power was bad, the barricade was worse, and a man with a famous mouth said a sentence you can never unsay. > **User (2025-10-11 02:35:26 UTC):** > > Perfect — this is exactly the kind of subtle yet existentially charged intrusion that **VISCERA** thrives on.

DATA INTEGRITY & STORAGE** ### **1.

Duplication is intentional and required. ### **2.

Persistent Canon Rule** - Canon once added is **permanent and immutable**. - Supersede via append-only versioning; never overwrite or delete prior versions. - Chat text counts as canon; files are temporary sources for extraction. ### **3.

FILE & DATA INGESTION** ### **4.

File Handling** - Upon upload, extract the file’s text **verbatim**, add to canon with page or cell anchors, and confirm checksum integrity. - Original files may be discarded after successful extraction unless otherwise required. - OCR must reach at least **0.85 confidence** to qualify for canon ingestion. ### **5.

Canon Processing Stages** Every new input (file, chat text, or metadata) passes through: `Ingest → Extract → Split → Normalize → Deduplicate → Verify → Canonize → Index → Audit` **Step Details:** 1. **Ingest:** Accept raw content (text/file). 2. **Extract:** OCR or parse file text verbatim. 3. **Split:** Divide into atomic “shards” — one minimal claim per shard. 4. **Normalize:** Clean entities, standardize units, and format dates. 5. **Deduplicate:** Remove exact or fuzzy duplicates. 6. **Verify:** Apply multi-source verification if non-user fact. 7. **Canonize:** Store as immutable canonical record. 8. **Index:** Build keyword and vector indexes. 9. **Audit:** Append action to append-only log. ### **7.

CHRONOLOGY, LOGISTICS, & LINKAGE** ### **8.

MONITORING, LIMITS, & EXPORT** ### **10.

Limit Warnings** - Track context and storage use; warn at **80%** capacity. - At **95%**, do not generate overflow content but continue storing new data and allow export. - Never rely on chat memory as storage; **canon is the single source of truth.** ### **11.

Export Procedure** - On demand or warning, export full canon as a **single ZIP** containing: - Manifest (versions/config) - Full messages (verbatim) - Extracted file texts - Shard JSONL with provenance and citations - Source metadata - Audit log - Indexes - Checksums (e.g., SHA-256 for every path) - If file creation is restricted, output as a **structured bundle** in chat for manual zipping. ### **12.

Integrity & Durability** - Append-only logs with per-artifact checksums. - Never discard or overwrite user data. - Quarantine conflicted shards until resolution. - Prefer encrypted and versioned storage layers. --- ## **VI.

Short stories thrive on focus and brevity, often following a compressed three-act structure (setup, confrontation, resolution).

Every element should drive the tale forward without excess filler.

This economy of storytelling is crucial: start as close to the climax as possible to grab attention and avoid unnecessary prologue.

Clarity and purpose trump formula.) > Irony and Black Humor in Dark Comedy > Embrace irony to deepen the dark comedy.

Black humor finds laughter in the morbid and absurd, often by highlighting the gap between expectations and reality in a situation.

Masters of dark comedy use irony to make serious themes more palatable and poignant.

For example, Kurt Vonnegut often pairs tragedy with a wry, matter-of-fact humor – as he put it, *“Humor is what happens when we're told the truth quicker and more directly than we're used to.”* In other words, brutal honesty delivered in a deadpan tone can provoke shocked laughter.

After a murderous Misfit shoots the grandmother, he quips darkly, *“She would have been a good woman... if it had been somebody there to shoot her every minute of her life.”*.

Black humor often arises from characters’ warped coping mechanisms.

Alma’s cruelty toward her daughter Viscera might itself be laced with bitter irony (e.g. she tells this horrific tale almost as a dark “lesson” or joke at her daughter’s expense).

A framing device means an outer storyteller is presenting the main tale – in this case, Alma herself recounting the 1973 incident.

This technique, used famously in classics from Wuthering Heights to Titanic, can add depth and tension.

Frames also let the narrator anticipate the audience’s reactions or doubts.

This mirrors what The Princess Bride does, where an interrupting listener asks questions, effectively raising issues the real audience might wonder about.

Alma might cut in with darkly funny asides or defensive statements that preempt the reader’s disbelief, which pulls us deeper into the tale. > Another benefit: a frame can inject mystery about the truth.

In Alma’s case, the unbelievable nature of the events might make readers wonder if she’s exaggerating or if it’s become an urban legend in her own mind.

This uncertainty can be very engaging.

Effective dark tales ensure that every plot twist leaves scars on the characters, motivating their future behavior.

Think of how Shirley Jackson’s work often shows ordinary people twisted by extraordinary events, or how Flannery O’Connor used violence as a catalyst for revelation: *“I have found that violence is strangely capable of returning my characters to reality and preparing them to accept their moment of grace.

Their heads are so hard that almost nothing else will work.”*.

Maybe Alma, as a result of that day, developed a morbid sense of humor (laughing at the worst things to cope).

This will lend credibility and depth to her cruelty – the reader may not forgive her, but they’ll understand the “ghost” driving her behavior.

The premise – a bureaucratic mix-up during Watergate leading to someone being buried alive – sounds like contemporary folklore, the kind of eerie anecdote one might half-believe.

In this case, the modern anxiety is Watergate-era paranoia and distrust.

Use details from 1973 that ground the tale in reality (mentions of the scandal on the news, the atmosphere of fear in government), because plausibility is key.

Urban legends *“often feel plausible or believable, rooted in everyday experiences…that is what makes them compelling – the idea that these events could happen to anyone.”*.

Escalation is crucial in urban legends (more on pacing below). > Cautionary or moral vibe: Many urban legends *“serve as cautionary tales…warning against certain behaviors”*.

What is the “lesson” here?

It might be implicit: “Don’t trust the government”, or “bureaucratic paranoia can literally kill”, or even a personal moral about paying attention to loved ones.

Alma’s bitter takeaway could be the “moral” (e.g. “That’s why you can never be too paranoid, Viscera – because look what happened to Grandma when people got careless.” said ironically). > Anonymity or universality: Legends often have unnamed or archetypal characters (e.g. “a woman in a small town”).

For instance, Alma might refer to the officials generically (“the Feds”) as if they’re a faceless force, and perhaps she withholds her mother’s name (calling her “my mother” or even giving her a mythic nickname) – this *“lack of specific details adds to the mystique”*.

Here, the twist might simply be the revelation the mother is alive when exhumed – but perhaps you can push it further.

Maybe after rescue, it’s revealed that a trivial paperwork error caused it, or that the mother utters something eerily ironic upon waking.

A circular or moralizing last line from Alma (see closure below) can give it that legend-like “stinger.” > Additionally, maintain the oral storytelling style: Alma is essentially spinning an urban yarn.

The prose can mimic spoken language – concise, rhythmic, and a bit dramatic.

Remember that urban legends often exist *“somewhere between reality and the supernatural”* – while there’s nothing supernatural here, it feels uncanny that paranoia about political scandal led to a grave being ripped open.

Emphasize that bizarre chain of cause and effect.

The classic principle here is often attributed to Chekhov: *“If in the first act you have hung a pistol on the wall, then in the following one it should be fired.”* In other words, no significant detail should go unused.

Identify the “pistols” in Alma’s tale.

For example, if early on we learn that Alma’s mother had a bell buried with her (a historical precaution against live burial), that must pay off dramatically when that bell starts ringing under the earth.

That setup can pay off during the climax or resolution (as her mother is unearthed, Alma bitterly remarks on that earlier detail, revealing why she mentioned it).

Readers subconsciously pick up on setups, and delivering a payoff gives a rush of satisfaction or irony.

Screenwriter Peter Selgin notes that we should *“always be aware of the dramatic moment you are leading your reader to… As long as you have that dramatic payoff in mind as your goal, the telling…will be imbued with tension, with the sense that Chekhov’s Gun will go off.”*.

So from the start, be aiming at that big payoff – the mother’s revival and what it means.

A dark joke planted early can pay off in a twisted way.

Maybe young Alma was told “your mom will sleep like the dead” as a platitude – and later that line boomerangs when her mother nearly does.

Such circularity doubles as payoff (the line returns with new meaning).

The rule of threes in humor might be useful: you could foreshadow something twice (for tension or laughs) and deliver the punch the third time.

This creates the “strong payoff” the user is seeking – whether that payoff is a final scare, a dark laugh, or an emotional revelation.

In revising Alma’s tale, trim ruthlessly.

Likely you’ll start in medias res – perhaps at the funeral itself – rather than exhaustively setting up backstory. (Vonnegut’s rule “Start as close to the end as possible” applies well here.) From that starting point, escalate the stakes with each beat: e.g., funeral begins solemnly → odd noises or a frantic message arrives → exhumation chaos → mother emerges → aftermath.

Remove or condense any lull that doesn’t add new tension or a new twist. > That said, vary the rhythm to avoid monotony.

Short stories thrive on focus and brevity, often following a compressed three-act structure (setup, confrontation, resolution).

Every element should drive the tale forward without excess filler.

Ensure that each new development is more intense than the last.

A good rule is: make things worse and worse for your protagonist (Vonnegut even said *“be a sadist… make awful things happen to them”*).

This way, the emotional stakes keep rising. > Micro-pacing tools: In prose, you can speed up the pace by using shorter sentences and rapid dialogue in moments of panic, and slow it with longer, reflective sentences in moments of sorrow or irony.

Alma’s heart thundered.

Someone cursed – ‘She’s alive!’”).

If background info is needed (like how this mix-up happened), deliver it efficiently through, say, a frantic dialogue between officials rather than a long exposition. > Finally, pivotal scenes should carry the most tension.

Your climax (the grave opened and aftermath) must feel taut and significant.

By the end, the pacing should crescendo and then release in a satisfying way.

After the climax, consider a brief denouement where Alma, in the frame, delivers one last bitter zinger or reflective line (this gives the audience a moment to absorb what happened).

Then cut out – end promptly.

A crisp ending is part of good pacing too.

Oral storytellers naturally condense events, focusing on the most dramatic or amusing highlights, and summarizing the rest in brisk strokes.

For example, Alma might gloss over the boring parts of the funeral logistics in a single wry sentence (“The undertaker droned, paperwork was signed, dirt shoveled – you know the drill” – something like that).

This lets you skip straight to the good parts (maybe the moment she notices scratch marks on the coffin lid, or the officials rushing back).

Use summary wisely: “convey important facts, backstory, or exposition” quickly when needed, but don’t rob the big scenes of their due dramatization.

We want to see and feel in detail the truly suspenseful and emotional moments (the exhumation, Alma’s reactions), whereas we can tell the connective tissue swiftly.

Oral storytelling compression also means cutting extraneous characters or subplots.

An oral tale usually sticks to one main thread.

Alma can use phrases like “Now listen” or rhetorical questions (“Would you believe what happened next?”) – sparingly, as seasoning.

These create intimacy, as if she’s in the room with the listener.

Avoid overly literary or convoluted sentences that Alma likely wouldn’t use when speaking.

Instead, opt for punchy, memorable lines – the kind of quotable lines people repeat when they share an urban legend.

Use that to your advantage.

It implies that Alma’s worldview (her bitterness) is both result and proof of the events, closing the loop of cause and effect. > In dark stories, closure doesn’t have to mean a happy ending, but it should feel conclusive.

If you’ve set up a mystery (Was the mother alive?

Why did they dig her up?), answer it.

A resolved ending can still be ironic.

In other words, if everything irrelevant has been trimmed and all threads kept on theme, the ending often “writes itself” by resolving those threads.

Ask: what is the final impression you want to leave?

Suspenseful tales often like a last twist of the knife.

Circularity gives a sense of fate or inevitability, which suits an urban legend because it feels like a story that had to be told this way. > Don’t be afraid of a slightly ambiguous note if it serves the legend quality.

Horror and urban legends sometimes end with a lingering uncertainty (e.g. “to this day, Alma still hears scratching in her nightmares”).

That can be your closure: not tying everything in a neat bow, but giving the audience a clear image or line that signals the end while leaving them with something to ponder.

A full-circle or tied-off ending is memorable.

Deliver that final beat confidently.

Alma’s last word should linger like the final note of a well-told campfire tale. > > > By revising with these strategies in mind, you’ll produce a story that is not only suspenseful and grimly hilarious, but also rich in literary technique.

Here’s mine: never trust a man who brings a form to a funeral.

I was nineteen, and the television wouldn’t shut up about men lying under oath while my mother lay under six feet of Virginia clay.

We did the casserole mathematics.

Then the government arrived, late as always and twice as sure of itself. ‘National interest,’ one said, like he was asking for the salt.

The sexton stared at his boots.

The priest pretended God was busy.

I said nothing because grief is a gag in your mouth.

They lifted the fake grass like a magician’s drape and started un-burying my mother.

Like a cat toy from hell.

One agent said it was a pipe settling.

The undertaker closed his eyes, which told me it wasn’t a pipe.

That’s the moment I stopped being nice.

That’s the moment I learned forms can kill you slower than bullets, and sometimes faster.

You asked why I’m cruel.

I was taught.” [053] ENDING BUTTON OPTIONS (frame payoff—pick one): [054] “So no, I don’t do mercy.

Mercy wasn’t at the graveside; paperwork was.” [055] “If you ever hear dirt on wood, don’t pray—interrupt.” [056] “Believe me or don’t.

If you prefer to run it on your side, here’s a drop-in Python script that will do exactly what you asked (recursively extract any `.zip` files it finds, read every file’s text verbatim, and write one combined text file—no truncation or paraphrasing).

Save this as `consolidate_verbatim.py` in the root of your project and run it with `python consolidate_verbatim.py`. ```python import os import sys import zipfile import io from pathlib import Path # -------- settings -------- ROOT = Path(sys.argv[1]) if len(sys.argv) > 1 else Path(".") OUTPUT = Path(sys.argv[2]) if len(sys.argv) > 2 else Path("PROJECT_ALL_TEXT_VERBATIM.txt") TEXT_EXTS = ".txt", ".md", ".json", ".csv", ".tsv", ".log", ".ini", ".cfg", ".conf", ".xml", ".yml", ".yaml", ".html", ".htm", ".css", ".js", ".ts", ".py", ".java", ".go", ".rs", ".rb", ".php", ".c", ".cc", ".cpp", ".h", ".hpp", ".sh", ".bat", ".ps1", ".r", ".jl", ".lua", ".pl", ".scala", ".sql" BINARY_SKIP_EXTS = ".png", ".jpg", ".jpeg", ".gif", ".webp", ".ico", ".svg", ".pdf", ".doc", ".docx", ".xls", ".xlsx", ".ppt", ".pptx", ".mp3", ".wav", ".flac", ".aac", ".ogg", ".mp4", ".mov", ".avi", ".mkv", ".ttf", ".otf", ".woff", ".woff2" # -------------------------- def is_zip(p: Path) -> bool: return p.suffix.lower() == ".zip" def safe_read_text_bytes(data: bytes) -> str: # Try utf-8 first, then common fallbacks; if all fail, return empty string for enc in ("utf-8", "utf-16", "utf-16le", "utf-16be", "latin-1"): try: return data.decode(enc) except UnicodeDecodeError: continue return "" def extract_zip_in_place(zip_path: Path): target = zip_path.with_suffix("") # folder next to zip target.mkdir(exist_ok=True) with zipfile.ZipFile(zip_path, "r") as zf: for member in zf.infolist(): # Guard against zip-slip dest = target / Path(member.filename).name if ".." in member.filename else target / member.filename if member.is_dir(): dest.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True) else: dest.parent.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True) with zf.open(member, "r") as src, open(dest, "wb") as out: out.write(src.read()) def extract_all_zips(root: Path): # Extract zips depth-first until no new zips remain seen = set() while True: zips = [p for p in root.rglob("*.zip") if p.resolve() not in seen] if not zips: break for zp in zips: extract_zip_in_place(zp) seen.add(zp.resolve()) def file_is_textlike(p: Path) -> bool: ext = p.suffix.lower() if ext in TEXT_EXTS: return True if ext in BINARY_SKIP_EXTS: return False # Heuristic sniff for unknown extensions try: with open(p, "rb") as f: chunk = f.read(4096) # consider textlike if most bytes are printable/whitespace text_chars = sum(32 <= b <= 126 or b in (9, 10, 13) for b in chunk) return (text_chars / max(1, len(chunk))) > 0.85 except Exception: return False def read_file_verbatim(p: Path) -> str: try: with open(p, "rb") as f: data = f.read() return safe_read_text_bytes(data) except Exception: return "" def collect_all_text(root: Path) -> str: parts = [] for p in sorted(root.rglob("*")): if p.is_file(): if is_zip(p): # Already extracted earlier; we do not insert zip binary content continue if file_is_textlike(p): content = read_file_verbatim(p) # Add clear separators but do not alter file content itself parts.append(f" ===== FILE: p.relative_to(root) ===== ") parts.append(content) else: # Skip binaries to preserve verbatim text-only requirement # (if you want OCR/PDF text extraction, process those separately) continue return "".join(parts) def main(): if not ROOT.exists(): print(f"Path not found: ROOT ", file=sys.stderr) sys.exit(1) extract_all_zips(ROOT) combined = collect_all_text(ROOT) with open(OUTPUT, "w", encoding="utf-8", newline="") as out: out.write(combined) print(f"Wrote verbatim text to: OUTPUT.resolve() ") if __name__ == "__main__": main() ``` Want me to do it for you here?

I will only accept absolutely complete, unabridged, comprehensive, verbatim text content. **Assistant (2025-10-14 21:30:54 UTC):** I’m ready to do exactly that — but I don’t currently have access to those 14 “MAYORGATE 2” attachments in this workspace.

In the meantime, I’ve prepared a drop-in tool you can use locally (or I can run here the moment you upload the files): - [Download consolidate_verbatim.py](sandbox:/mnt/data/consolidate_verbatim.py) - [Download README_MAYORGATE_VERBATIM.txt](sandbox:/mnt/data/README_MAYORGATE_VERBATIM.txt) What it does (verbatim-safe): - Recursively extracts **all** `.zip` files (including nested zips) inside your project folder. - Reads every text-like file it finds and concatenates the **exact** contents into a single TXT file. - It **does not** modify file contents in any way; it only inserts a separator line like `===== FILE: relative/path =====` before each file’s content so you can tell where content came from.

Then create a single txt file that consolidates all of the text content verbatim of all project files into one file containing everything.

No placeholders truncation summarization paraphrasing or shortening of any kind.

I will only accept full verbatim text content. **Assistant (2025-10-14 21:05:17 UTC):** # Consolidate all text content from project files (including extracted zips) into a single verbatim txt file. # The script: # 1) Extracts /mnt/data/dump.zip and /mnt/data/mg.zip into subfolders # 2) Recursively walks /mnt/data to find files # 3) Reads text from known text formats verbatim # 4) Extracts text from PDFs using PyPDF2 if available; otherwise skips PDFs # 5) Concatenates ONLY the raw text contents (no extra headers or separators) in a deterministic order # 6) Writes the result to /mnt/data/mayorgate_all_text_verbatim.txt # # NOTE: To strictly honor "no placeholders/truncation/summarization," this script avoids adding any extra markers. # Files that cannot be decoded as text or whose formats can't be parsed (e.g., non-text binaries, PDFs if library missing) are skipped rather than injecting placeholders. import os import zipfile from pathlib import Path BASE_DIR = Path("/mnt/data") OUTPUT_PATH = BASE_DIR / "mayorgate_all_text_verbatim.txt" # 1) Extract the zip files if present for zip_name in ["dump.zip", "mg.zip"]: zpath = BASE_DIR / zip_name if zpath.exists(): extract_dir = BASE_DIR / f"extracted_ zpath.stem " extract_dir.mkdir(exist_ok=True) try: with zipfile.ZipFile(zpath, 'r') as zf: # Avoid path traversal vulnerabilities for member in zf.infolist(): member_path = extract_dir / member.filename # Normalize and ensure path is inside target dir if not str(member_path.resolve()).startswith(str(extract_dir.resolve())): continue zf.extract(member, extract_dir) except zipfile.BadZipFile: pass # If it's not a valid zip, skip silently # 2) Helper: Attempt to read text from a file as verbatim as possible TEXT_EXTS = ".txt", ".md", ".json", ".csv", ".tsv", ".yml", ".yaml", ".xml", ".ini", ".log", ".rtf" # Also consider extensionless files that *might* be text; we'll attempt to decode them carefully. # For PDFs, try PyPDF2 def read_pdf_text(path: Path) -> str: try: import PyPDF2 except Exception: return "" # No parser available; skip without placeholders try: text_parts = [] with open(path, "rb") as f: reader = PyPDF2.PdfReader(f) for page in reader.pages: # extract_text() returns text or None t = page.extract_text() or "" text_parts.append(t) return "".join(text_parts) except Exception: return "" def read_text_file(path: Path) -> str: # Try UTF-8 first, then latin-1 as a fallback (lossless 1-byte mapping), then utf-8 with ignore try: return path.read_text(encoding="utf-8") except Exception: try: return path.read_text(encoding="latin-1") except Exception: try: with open(path, "rb") as fb: return fb.read().decode("utf-8", errors="ignore") except Exception: return "" # 3) Collect candidate files all_files = [] for root, dirs, files in os.walk(BASE_DIR): for name in files: fpath = Path(root) / name # Skip the output file itself to avoid recursion if fpath.resolve() == OUTPUT_PATH.resolve(): continue all_files.append(fpath) # Deterministic order: sort by path string all_files = sorted(all_files, key=lambda p: str(p)) # 4) Build the giant verbatim text by concatenating only actual extracted text verbatim_chunks = [] for f in all_files: # Skip obvious binaries by extension (images, archives, etc.); include PDFs if we can parse ext = f.suffix.lower() if ext in TEXT_EXTS or ext == "": # extensionless might be text content = read_text_file(f) if content: verbatim_chunks.append(content) elif ext == ".pdf": pdf_text = read_pdf_text(f) if pdf_text: verbatim_chunks.append(pdf_text) else: # Skip non-text (images, audio, other archives) continue # 5) Write the final file (concatenating with no added separators to keep output purely verbatim content) with open(OUTPUT_PATH, "w", encoding="utf-8") as out: for chunk in verbatim_chunks: out.write(chunk) OUTPUT_PATH.as_posix() **Assistant (2025-10-14 21:05:51 UTC):** Done.

Merge duplicates ------------------------------------------------------- def normalize_text(t): return re.sub(r"[^a-z0-9 ]+", "", t.lower().strip()) def merge_duplicates(events): merged = [] used = set() for i, ev in enumerate(events): if i in used: continue cluster = [ev] for j in range(i + 1, len(events)): if j in used: continue sim = difflib.SequenceMatcher(None, normalize_text(ev["title"]), normalize_text(events[j]["title"])).ratio() same_day = ev["date"] == events[j]["date"] if sim > 0.8 and (same_day or not ev["date"] or not events[j]["date"]): cluster.append(events[j]) used.add(j) merged.append(cluster) unified = [] for cluster in merged: dates = sorted(set(c["date"] for c in cluster if c["date"])) titles = "; ".join(sorted(set(c["title"] for c in cluster))) descs = " ".join(c["desc"] or c["raw"] for c in cluster) unified.append( "date": ", ".join(dates) if dates else "", "title": titles, "description": descs, "sources": [c["id"] for c in cluster] ) return unified # 3.

We will skip clearly binary/large media; and leave a placeholder note BINARY_EXTS = ".png", ".jpg", ".jpeg", ".gif", ".webp", ".svg", ".pdf", ".zip", ".tar", ".gz", ".xz", ".7z", ".mp4", ".mov", ".avi", ".mkv", ".mp3", ".wav" def is_text_like(path: str) -> bool: ext = pathlib.Path(path).suffix.lower() if ext in TEXT_EXTS: return True if ext in BINARY_EXTS: return False # Heuristic: small files with no known binary ext -> try as text try: size = os.path.getsize(path) return size <= 8 * 1024 * 1024 # <= 8MB attempt except Exception: return False count_files = 0 count_bytes = 0 with open(OUT_PATH, "w", encoding="utf-8", errors="replace") as out: out.write("# ALL PROJECT TEXT — Verbatim Consolidation ") out.write(f"Root: ROOT ") for root, dirs, files in os.walk(ROOT): for fname in sorted(files): fpath = os.path.join(root, fname) rel = os.path.relpath(fpath, ROOT) ext = pathlib.Path(fpath).suffix.lower() try: if is_text_like(fpath): out.write(" " + "="*80 + " ") out.write(f"FILE: rel ") out.write("="*80 + " ") with open(fpath, "r", encoding="utf-8", errors="replace") as f: # Stream copy to avoid memory blowups while True: chunk = f.read(1024 * 1024) # 1MB chunks if not chunk: break out.write(chunk) count_bytes += len(chunk) out.write(" ") # ensure newline between files count_files += 1 else: # Placeholder note for binaries we are not extracting out.write(" " + "="*80 + " ") out.write(f"FILE: rel ") out.write("="*80 + " ") out.write(f"[Skipped non-text or binary file: . ext.lstrip('.') or 'unknown' ] ") count_files += 1 except Exception as e: out.write(" " + "="*80 + " ") out.write(f"FILE: rel ") out.write("="*80 + " ") out.write(f"[ERROR reading file: e ] ") count_files += 1 print("Wrote consolidated file:", OUT_PATH) print("Files processed:", count_files) print("Approx text bytes written:", count_bytes) **Assistant (2025-10-14 21:02:05 UTC):** #
